### Page 17

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

17 
ODI growth in CEE may come not only from more firms investing abroad, but also from 
scaling-up of existing foreign investment by current CEE multinationals. 
Evidence from Poland suggests that firms increasingly perceive international 
expansion as a prerequisite for further growth. In our report prepared for the European 
Economic Congress in Katowice, in-depth interviews with 30 Polish business leaders 
indicates that Polish companies increasingly view international expansion not as an 
option, but as a strategic necessity. The main insights from the report can be 
summarized as follows:  
1) Internationalisation is a prerequisite for sustained growth as domestic market can no 
longer provide the scale needed to justify major investment in automation, 
digitalisation and advanced production. “Foreign expansion is now a necessity, not 
an option.” (Grzegorz Rychwalski, Vice-President, Polish Association of Employers of 
the Pharmaceutical Industry). 
2) Acquiring foreign businesses provides customers, brands and local capabilities. 
Partnerships with global companies offer a bridge for firms without recognised 
international brands. “I already see greater scope, for example, for acquisitions of 
German companies.” (Dariusz Grzeszczak, President, Erbud). 
3) Scale is the missing link: preferential SME treatment may create a growth trap. 
Support should reward productivity and provide instruments for mature companies 
undertaking strategic cross-border projects. “The greatest impact comes from 
entities that operate on a global scale.” (Janusz Zakręcki, President, PZL Mielec). 
4) Economic diplomacy should be coordinated: domestic development institutions are 
helpful, but the support landscape remains fragmented and complex. Mature firms 
pursuing major contracts or acquisitions need a single rapid route to finance, 
insurance and diplomatic support that bring domestic supply chains into overseas 
infrastructure and strategic projects. “The most effective model of expansion is to 
operate as a general contractor.” (Piotr Kańtoch, Vice-President, Grupa Powen-
Wafapomp).  
5) Conservative financing limits the pace of expansion. Reliance on retained earnings 
and public grants or preferential financing from the EU strengthens resilience but can 
slow investment, acquisitions and international scaling. Companies need broader use 
of risk capital, long-term debt, export credit and transaction insurance, supported by 
stronger financial capabilities. Public instruments should crowd in commercial 
finance rather than replace it where market funding is available. 
Takeaways 
The main takeway of our analysis is that ODI could become an increasingly important 
engine of CEE growth. Over the past three decades, inward FDI and exports have been 
powerful engines of convergence, but the next stage will increasingly require successful 
domestic firms to scale internationally. In CEE countries ODI remains low relative to the 
level of economic development, despite a growing number of firms with the capabilities 
to operate abroad. Moving towards Stage IV of the IDP would therefore represent a 
natural evolution of the CEE growth model, complementing the benefits of inward FDI 
with those generated by domestic multinationals. 
For policymakers, the key takeaway is that ODI can be supported by reducing fixed 
costs of entering foreign markets. CEE countries do not need to subsidise capital 
outflows or select national champions. Instead, successful policy would reduce the fixed 
costs and risks that discourage competitive firms from investing abroad. Existing export-
promotion infrastructure provides a strong starting point, but support could be 
increasingly extended beyond exports to acquisitions, greenfield projects and foreign

### Page 18

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

18 
subsidiaries. This requires efficient market intelligence, partner identification, financing 
and risk-sharing instruments, legal and regulatory assistance, and coordinated 
economic diplomacy. The gradual shift already visible in Poland indicates that the 
region's institutional architecture is beginning to move in this direction. 
For firms, the key takeaway is that going global can provide an important avenue for 
further growth. The most productive CEE firms can benefit from international expansion 
through access to new customers, technologies, brands and know-how, as well as 
greater economies of scale. Successful expansion, however, requires a more 
international management mindset, careful identification of commercially viable 
opportunities, and an appropriate financing strategy.  
Ultimately, the transition towards a more internationally oriented CEE growth model 
will have to be driven by firms themselves. Governments can create favourable 
conditions for outward expansion, but they cannot determine which firms should invest 
abroad or where the best opportunities lie. These decisions must remain with 
entrepreneurs, managers and investors responding to market incentives. As CEE 
economies mature and domestic firms accumulate competitive advantages, 
international expansion is likely to become an increasingly natural route to greater 
scale, productivity and innovation. The next stage of CEE convergence will therefore 
depend not only on the region’s continued ability to attract foreign multinationals, 
but increasingly on its ability to develop successful multinationals of its own.

### Page 19

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

19 
EMEA FX, rates and debt strategy 
EMEA economies have weathered the US-Iran supply shock better than expected, with 
the CEE3 growth forecast at 2.8% this year and 3.0% next year. Consumption remains 
the main driver, although slowing real wage growth and higher inflation will 
increasingly constrain households. Food and household energy costs are set to lift 
CEE3 headline and core inflation next year, while Romania and Turkey should 
continue to disinflate from high levels. Monetary policy will diverge: the CNB is 
expected to hike again in November, the NBP to start tightening in early 2027, while 
the NBH, NBR and CBT retain scope to cut. The global backdrop remains hostile for 
EMEA assets, reflecting a strong US dollar, high energy prices, geopolitical uncertainty 
and further Fed and ECB tightening.  
Macro outlook: Growth holds, price pressures build 
EMEA economies have remained resilient to the supply shock triggered by the US-Iran 
conflict in March. Although activity has slowed, the downturn has been much milder 
than initially expected given the region’s heavy dependence on energy and imports.  ur 
economists forecast GDP-weighted CEE3 growth of 2.8% this year and 3.0% next year, 
led by household consumption, while industrial sentiment remains broadly stable. 
Hungary has caught up with Poland and the Czech Republic despite global headwinds, 
but domestic pressures and fiscal consolidation continue to weigh on Romania, where 
we expect a mild 0.5% recession this year followed by 2.2% growth next year. Turkey is 
also losing momentum, particularly in domestic demand, although exports should 
support growth of 2.8% this year before an acceleration to 4.5% next year. PMI data 
indicates the region’s resilience; with the exception of Turkey readings have improved 
from their pre-conflict February levels in all countries, pointing to continued strength in 
the months ahead. 
Fig 8 PMIs indicate continued economic resilience  Fig 9 Retail sales (January 2025 = 100)

Source: Macrobond, ING  Source: Macrobond, ING

EMEA labour markets have eased over the past year, slowing wage growth. Real wage 
growth is gradually decelerating across the CEE3 and Turkey, weakening a key driver of 
household consumption. Together with higher inflation, this is likely to constrain 
spending. However, CEE3 savings rates remain only slightly below 2020 levels, giving 
households some capacity to maintain consumption and absorb price shocks, including 
those linked to US-Iran tensions. Romania is the regional exception: real wage growth 
turned negative in recent months and returned to zero only in September, although this 
should support a recovery in consumption next year. 
CEE3 inflation is currently driven mainly by fuel prices, while unexpectedly weak food 
inflation has limited the rise in headline rates. We expect this to reverse in the coming 
months. Global agricultural commodity prices are at their highest since 2022, and local

harvest data and producer prices point to an imminent increase in food inflation. El Niño 
adds another risk, although its historical impact on EMEA has been smaller than 
elsewhere. The pass-through from higher household energy costs has also yet to reach 
CPI; it typically takes 6–12 months and is often concentrated in January because of 
fixed-term contracts. We therefore expect both headline and core inflation to peak next 
year. Average inflation should rise from 2.7% this year to 3.1% next year before easing 
to 2.3% in 2028. Romania and Turkey remain idiosyncratic cases. In Romania, the base-
effect spike from tax changes linked to fiscal consolidation has passed, and inflation 
should fall from 8.5% this year to 4.7% next year.  n Turkey, EMEA’s most energy-
sensitive economy, disinflation stalled earlier this year but has recently resumed; we 
expect average inflation to decline from 30.8% this year to 25.0% next year. 
Fiscal policy has come under renewed pressure to loosen as the US-Iran conflict has 
increased demand for measures to offset high fuel prices and fund greater defence 
spending. Even so, we expect only a modest widening of deficits next year in the Czech 
Republic and Turkey, both of which start from much lower levels than elsewhere in 
EMEA. Hungary and Romania, by contrast, are continuing visible fiscal consolidation. 
Poland’s deficit is set to remain unchanged at the highest level in the region, with next 
November’s general election posing an upside risk. 
Fig 10 Wage growth in CEE region slowing down (%, YoY)  Fig 11 Food inflation coming back

Source: Macrobond, ING  Source: Macrobond, ING

Monetary policy has changed little this year, with central banks awaiting clearer 
evidence on inflationary pressure from the US-Iran conflict and the related supply shock. 
The CNB is the only central bank to have tightened so far, and our economists expect 
another 25bp hike in November, with a growing risk of further increases depending on 
January repricing. The NBP remains relatively dovish; we expect its first hike only in the 
first quarter of 2027, with 50bp of tightening in total. Given their distinct circumstances 
and much higher starting rates, the NBH and NBR are still expected to cut by 125bp and 
50bp, respectively, although upside inflation surprises could reduce the scope for easing. 
The CBT paused its cutting cycle after the US-Iran conflict began, but recent 
developments point to a resumption, with 200bp of cuts expected this year and a 
further 500bp next year.

Local Markets Strategy: Staying selective amid a 
hostile global backdrop 
EMEA is particularly exposed to higher energy prices because of its heavy reliance on 
imports. Energy and food also carry large weights in CPI, increasingly clouding the 
inflation outlook following the double-digit inflation shock of 2022. Our global 
assumptions of continued US dollar strength, persistently high energy prices, ongoing 
geopolitical uncertainty and further ECB and Fed rate hikes, add to the pressure on EM 
currencies. Signs of stress in the eurozone bond market further weaken the backdrop, 
leaving the EMEA environment increasingly hostile to risk-taking. 
This backdrop is evident in the sharp repricing at the front end of yield curves, as 
markets have partly shifted towards a 2021–22-style scenario of aggressive central-
bank tightening. Although our economists do not see the situations as identical, the 
longer the US-Iran conflict lasts, the more closely the current environment resembles 
the previous hiking cycle. Front-end rates remain driven mainly by energy prices, while 
central-bank guidance has had little influence on pricing. Markets imply substantially 
more tightening than our economists forecast, but without a meaningful global  
de-escalation it is difficult to take the opposite view.  
 n the bond space, higher inflation, households’ price caps and weaker growth all pose 
upside risks to fiscal deficits next year. The current global backdrop is generally 
unfavourable for duration, so we favour strong idiosyncratic stories.  ungary’s domestic 
story remains supportive. Experience elsewhere suggest the HGB spread could converge 
towards 50–120bp as ERM II entry approaches. It has just reached 200bp, with entry still 
more than two years away. TURKGBs, by contrast, need a more supportive global 
environment to draw buyers back despite cheaper valuations and the prospect of an 
earlier resumption of CBT rate cuts. ROMGBs have tightened to record levels versus CEE 
peers following positive political developments and now look expensive. CZGBs face a 
distinctly bearish mix next year: a wider fiscal deficit, record bond redemptions, likely 
exclusion from the GBI-EM index (a benchmark for local-currency emerging-market 
government bonds) and further tightening by a highly hawkish central bank. Poland has 
recently been downgraded and faces the risk of further downgrades next year. Its fiscal 
deficit is at a record high, the largest in the EU, and next November’s general election 
offers little prospect of improvement. Meanwhile, despite its dovish bias, the NBP has 
started discussing possible rate hikes.  
As with duration, the global backdrop is highly unfavourable for EMEA currencies. A 
strong US dollar is weighing on EM FX, while the prospect of further Fed and ECB rate 
hikes is eroding carry returns. We therefore favour currencies that continue to offer 
attractive carry (TRY and HUF) or support from a hawkish central bank (CZK). We avoid 
currencies combining economic imbalances, such as current account (CA) or fiscal 
deficits, with a dovish central bank (PLN and RON).

### Page 22

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

22 
Hard currency sovereign debt: 
Another test for sovereign spreads 
The current environment of elevated core rates, high energy prices and renewed 
geopolitical risks should pose another test for the resilience of EM credit spreads, 
which still remain close to all time tights despite some recent risk-off moves.  
2026 has been a tough year for fixed income assets, with the sharp move higher in UST 
yields on the back of a repricing of global central bank expectations acting as a 
significant drag on total returns in the credit markets. War in the Middle East has also 
disrupted another EM region, once seen as something of a ‘safe haven’ in the market. 
Despite this backdrop, EM sovereign credit markets have held up well – excess returns at 
the index level are modestly positive, with HY sovereigns outperforming and even 
generating positive total returns, thanks to their larger spread cushion and shorter 
duration profile. Weaker performers this year have understandably included GCC names 
such as the UAE and Bahrain that have faced trade disruption and geopolitical risk, but 
also lower-beta energy importers such as Indonesia and the Philippines. In CEE the 
change in government in Hungary has driven decent performance, while political 
uncertainty weighed more on Bulgaria and Romania. In CIS, Armenia and Uzbekistan 
have continued their strong performance, backed by fundamental progress.  
The move in core rates has been the key macro story, with the US 10Y spiking over 5.3% 
from below 4% in February and still hovering above the 5% level despite a rebound in 
recent weeks. The move in bunds has not been as extreme, but still around 50bp higher 
YTD at over 3.4%, while political noise and wider sovereign spreads in France have also 
become a topic of interest for CEE euro-denominated bond issuers.  
At the index level, EM sovereign spreads are pretty much back where they started the 
year, having made something of a round trip through this year’s volatility. The March 
selloff saw us 40bp wider, before a sharp rally of 60bp through June, then the latest risk-
off moves since late September – this still leaves us at levels tighter than any period 
since 2006/2007, and we expect further decompression is likely absent a significant shift 
in the global macro environment.  
The narrative from recent years still stands – all-in yields remain attractive at near 7%, 
while the improvement in EM fundamentals in recent years stands out against a 
backdrop of fiscal concerns and political noise in the DM world, somewhat blurring the 
lines for sovereign debt markets. We are somewhat cautious on higher-beta EM 
sovereigns, but don’t expect the global pressures to drive a more systemic crisis in EM.

### Page 23

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

23 
Key themes 
• Domestic political noise takes centre stage: Despite the Middle East conflict driving 
big swings in global markets, local political stories continue to be a key differentiator 
for EM sovereign performance. Elections in Hungary and Bulgaria have seen new 
governments, while the energy shock is yet to cause widespread protests as 
government action keeps the impact contained. Romania has had an extended 
period of interim government, but prospects are looking up for progress there. 
Looking ahead, we have elections for Serbia later this month, while the political cycle 
should be restarting for the likes of Poland and potentially Turkey.  
• EU relations in focus: With the energy shock placing upward pressure on inflation 
and likely driving fiscal strains, EU funding remains a key pillar of strength for CEE 
sovereigns. This year’s RRF deadline has increased the time pressure on EU funds 
absorption, while the SAFE instrument adds an extra resource. Optimism in Hungary 
has largely been driven by both the unlocking frozen EU funds and also a return of 
euro adoption to the agenda. In Bulgaria, euro adoption has been successfully 
implemented, while the EDP is a key pressure point on most of CEE for driving fiscal 
consolidation.  
• Rating moves still positive: While external conditions are adding a negative shock to 
fundamentals in CEE, the positive news is that the underlying trend was generally 
positive heading into the latest energy shock. In the higher quality space, Croatia 
saw another rating upgrade early this year, while an end to political deadlock in 
Bulgaria helped drive the outlook back to positive. Poland is more of an outlier here, 
with a downgrade coming from fiscal pressures. A lot of focus remains on Romania 
keeping hold of its IG ratings, with political progress a key part of the puzzle, while 
Hungary will hope to reverse its downward trend from recent years given the new 
government’s direction. The trend of improvement has been strong in CIS, while 
Turkey also temporarily saw another positive outlook at the start of the year.  
• Issuance slows as inflows dry up:  A busy start to the year saw heavy issuance for 
EM credit markets in January and February, which allowed for something of a 
breather in the early stages of the war in the Middle East. This theme continued later 
in the year, with heavy issuance through to the summer allowing for a somewhat 
quieter time than usual in September and October so far. One key part of this 
technical puzzle to keep an eye on is the clear slowdown in EM debt portfolio flows 
seen from tracking data in September, amid the surge in core rates. EM investors will 
be hoping this is just a temporary blip, as in March this year.

CEE country views 
(Moody’s/S&P/Fitch rating; ↑=positive, ↓=negative outlook) 
• BGARIA (Baa1/BBB+↑/BBB+↑): While Bulgaria entered this year with optimism from 
the official entry into the Eurozone, underperformance in the market has been driven 
by political uncertainty, given the snap elections seen in April, along with fiscal 
deterioration. With the political situation seemingly stabilised for now and a more 
realistic fiscal path being drawn out in terms of gradual consolidation after this 
year’s budget deficit widening, we see scope for an improvement in performance 
heading into 2027. Current valuations offer space for compression against the likes 
of Poland and Croatia, along with the potential to retighten against Eurozone hopeful 
Hungary.  
• CROATI (A3/A/A-): With a combination of political noise and fiscal pressures a key 
worry for much of Europe (both EM and DM), Croatia continues to stand out as 
something of a safe haven given its relative stability. Steady growth, a sharp 
reduction in government debt ratios (30% of GDP reduction since 2020) and solid EU 
inflows mean Croatia continues to see a strong fundamental profile. Valuations put 
the country as one of the tightest names in the EM space, and tighter than some of 
the more troubled names in Western Europe, with 5-year paper offering around 
20bp in z-spread, and around 60bp on the 10-year. This offers limited scope for 
further tightening, but Croatia should remain a solid low-beta option. 
• REPHUN (Baa2↓/BBB-↓/BBB↓): While the overall story remains positive for Hungary, 
we continue to see less value in the Eurobond market, relative to local markets. FX 
issuance for the year has been completed, while front-loading supply early in 2027 is 
likely. The composition of issuance could shift slightly more towards EUR as the country 
heads closer to euro adoption. As with local markets, a strong and credible budget plan 
could offer more scope for spreads to compress relative to higher-rated peers. But 
expectations are already elevated for significant consolidation to be announced, and 
valuations do not offer much scope for upside. REP UN €40s have seen spreads 
compress by 90bp YT  relative to BGAR A €38s, and the pickup for  ungary in general 
now is in the range of 10-20bp over Bulgaria and 20-40bp over Poland. 
• POLAND (A3/A-/A-↓): Poland is generally well placed to cope with the energy shock 
from the Middle East, compared to CEE peers.  owever, after Moody’s downgrade to 
A3, the focus shifts to the negative rating outlook at Fitch, which has come on the 
back of persistent fiscal pressures. Strong growth momentum and EU fund inflows 
mean patience is likely before downgrades lower the rating below the single A level, 
although noise will likely continue, especially if there is more political noise. 
Valuations look balanced, with the USD curve continuing to look more attractive than 
EUR, especially with issuance there likely out of the way for the year.  
• SERBIA (Ba2/BBB-/BB+↑): Despite some deterioration in recent years (wider fiscal 
deficit, drop off in F  ), Serbia’s fundamentals remain closer to higher-rated IG peers, 
and generally even stronger than most in the CEE region. The focus remains on pre-
election fiscal loosening and energy pressures on the external balance. However, 
politics remains the point of uncertainty, with upcoming elections bringing at a 
minimum the potential for further anti-government protests, and more uncertainty 
about the outcome than any time in the past decade.  
• TURKEY (Ba3/BB-/BB-): In the Eurobond space, Turkey has held up well despite the 
global macro volatility in recent months and local investment fund noise. We 
continue to see fundamentals as relatively stable against a backdrop of higher 
energy prices and global bond yields. CIS country views 
• ARMEN (Ba3↑/BB-↑/BB-↑): This year has seen a continuation of fundamental 
improvement for Armenia, with positive rating outlooks at all three agencies, and

### Page 25

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

25 
elections that passed without major surprises. While there remains the potential for 
further positive catalysts from the ongoing peace process with Azerbaijan, conflict in 
the Middle East leaves Armenia in a more vulnerable position, given the increasing 
reliance on external inflows. Valuations have cheapened up somewhat versus peers 
such as Uzbekistan and South Africa in recent months but continue to price in a 
further rating upgrade to mid BB level.  
• AZERBJ (Baa3↑/NR/BBB-): The fundamental picture is generally positive for 
Azerbaijan, with ratings now comfortably in the  G space and Moody’s holding a 
positive outlook. At the same time, the country stands to be a significant beneficiary 
from the current energy price spike, given its high sensitivity to oil prices in terms of 
fiscal and external accounts. While there remains some risk of negative spillovers 
from the conflict in the Middle East given the generally tense relations with Iran and 
cooperation with Israel, for now this seems well contained. The ongoing peace 
process with Armenia seems more likely to act as a positive catalyst at this stage, 
given the progress in recent years. In this context valuations look attractive for 
Azerbaijan, which should act as a strong defensive play in the event of a more 
prolonged conflict in the Middle East and persistent oil price spike. The current pickup 
over Kazakhstan appears to offer decent value, while the sovereign continues to 
benefit from lower geopolitical risk than oil exporting peer Oman.  
• KAZAKS (Baa1/BBB/BBB): Overall, the underlying fundamental momentum is 
generally positive, with fiscal consolidation under close focus, while higher energy 
prices should offer a modest boost to export and budget revenues. However, as with 
budget policy, on the issuance front, more quasi-sovereign supply remains a risk in 
the coming years given tight valuations even within the BBB context. Strong 
technicals keep the front-end well anchored, while long-end spreads now are 
broadly in line with higher-rated peers such as Qatar and the UAE.  
• UZBEK (Ba2/BB/BB↑): The fundamental picture for Uzbekistan has improved 
significantly in recent years, with narrowing twin deficits amid solid growth reflected 
in rating upgrades. Fitch’s positive outlook suggests there is still scope for 
improvement to continue, while the stronger credit profile should increase the 
country’s resilience to the current energy shock. An expected catch-up in gold 
exports, alongside ongoing capital inflows linked to the privatisation pipeline should 
keep the momentum strong. Against this backdrop, Uzbekistan remains the 
strongest fundamental story in the CIS region but current valuations don’t appear to 
offer a lot of excitement about potential spread tightening.

Armenia has shown remarkable resilience to the global and regional 
challenges of 2026, supported by renewed inflows of visitors, 
remittances and capital. Skilful foreign policy manoeuvring and solid 
finances provide additional support to sentiment and insulation to 
the balance of payments. However, the increasing reliance on 
external inflows is a watch factor, as persistent twin deficits and 
close economic links with Russia remain important vulnerabilities. 
Higher CPI risks stemming from domestic demand and external cost 
inputs have prompted the region’s first rate hike this year, with 
further upside risks in sight. While we remain constructive on 
Armenia’s macro outlook and assets, we see growing exposure to 
foreign politics-related downside risks, while the AMD appears 
increasingly expensive relative to fundamentals. *Quarterly data is eop, annual is average; shading denotes ING forecast data  
Source: National sources, ING estimates

GDP growth (% YoY) vs net cash transfer inflow (% of GDP)  Activity: Supported by the new wave of external inflows

GDP growth remains elevated at c.7% YoY on a 4Q rolling basis. 
Some softening in investment-heavy industries in 9M26 has been 
offset by stronger activity in consumption, financial services and 
real estate. Armenia is benefiting from additional visitor arrivals and 
financial inflows, with net remittances rising from a typical c.6% to 
c.9% of GDP. Around two-thirds of gross cash inflows originate in 
Russia, making it a key variable for Armenia’s growth outlook. 
Assuming foreign policy continuity, gradual moderation of GDP 
growth towards c.5% pa, alongside a stable c.3.5% budget deficit, 
remains our base case. A material disruption to Russia-related flows 
of people, goods (including Russia’s parallel imports) and capitals 
remains a downside risk and a factor to watch. Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation, households’ C I expectations* and key rate  Interest rates: CPI risks triggered a precautionary hike

Inflation has hovered around 4% YoY for most of 2026. Relatively 
low import exposure to developed markets and c.5% YoY dram 
appreciation have helped contain price pressures from strong 
domestic demand and higher global commodity prices. Meanwhile, 
the deteriorating global inflation outlook, combined with regional 
and domestic risks, has raised concerns over further inflationary 
pressures among households, corporates and policymakers. With 
CPI likely to remain above the 3% target for longer, the Central Bank 
of Armenia raised the policy rate by 25bp to 6.75% in September. 
We do not expect this to mark the start of a prolonged hiking cycle 
but still see the balance of rate risks as skewed upwards. 
Source: National sources, CEIC, ING; * % of households expecting high CPI   
/ 
Balance of payments, remittances and Armenian dram (AMD)  FX: Supported, but increasingly expensive

The dram has continued to show above-expected resilience to 
global capital volatility in 2026, as it is supported by rising capital 
inflows, remittances, comfortable reserves and favourable investor 
sentiment linked to the peace process with Azerbaijan and positive 
rating trajectory. This continues to offset Armenia’s sizeable 
current-account deficit, helping keep the currency stronger than 
implied by long-term valuation metrics. However, Armenia remains 
structurally dependent on continued external financial flows, while 
trade deficits and close economic links to Russia remain important 
vulnerabilities. We therefore expect the AMD to stay resilient to a 
potential global risk off in the near term but see limited room for 
further appreciation and continue to view longer-term risks as 
skewed towards gradual depreciation. Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Macro trend Political cycle Ratings FC LC 
Activity – Presidential: 2031 S&P NR NR 
Fiscal Neutral Parliamentary: 2029 Moody’s Baa3 Baa3 
Monetary Neutral Local: 2030 Fitch BBB- BBB-

Azerbaijan remains the CCA region’s financially strongest sovereign, 
supported by large and growing fiscal and monetary reserve assets, 
low public debt and a robust external balance sheet. Higher 
hydrocarbon prices have strengthened the near-term fiscal and 
external outlook, as well as domestic confidence in the non-oil 
sectors. However, weak oil production, limited diversification and 
subdued private investment continue to constrain potential growth. 
Inflation is manageable for now, supporting an extended policy hold, 
although imported price pressures through high exposure to trading 
partners with high risks of upward CPI surprises, warrant caution. We 
remain constructive on sovereign resilience and external stability 
but see structurally modest growth as the main medium-term 
challenge. *Quarterly data is eop, annual is average; shading denotes ING forecast data 
Source: National sources, ING estimates

GDP and oil/non-oil contribution (%YoY, ppt)  Activity: Recovering now, but long-term growth limited

GDP growth is currently into the second year of below-average 
growth, dragged down by an underperforming fuel sector at the 
beginning of the year, reflecting both structural and temporary 
factors. At the same time, higher hydrocarbon prices support 
financial inflows, which pass through to the wider economy, 
supporting confidence in the non-fuel sectors. Retail trade and non-
fuel industry output have been accelerating throughout 2026 amid 
faster lending activity. While the consolidated budget remains in 
surplus thanks to fuel revenues, the stable non-oil deficit supports 
domestic demand. Following the extended fuel-led slowdown of 
economic activity in 2025-26, we expect growth to recover in the 
medium term. However, the potential growth rate appears capped 
at 2-3% unless economic diversification accelerates. 
Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Key rate, consumer prices and producer prices  Monetary policy: CPI risks manageable, rates on hold

Inflation risks have recently shifted somewhat to the upside, as food 
prices and higher global transport and commodity costs have 
renewed pressure on Azerbaijan’s producer price index.  owever, 
the exchange-rate peg remains a strong nominal anchor, limiting 
pass-through to consumer prices. With CPI still within the 4±2% 
target range, we expect the refinancing rate to remain on hold over 
the medium term. However, further imported inflation linked to 
developments in the Middle East is the main upside risk, given 
Azerbaijan’s trade exposure to the eurozone and other economies 
sensitive to global cost input pressures. For now, further policy 
adjustment appears unnecessary unless price pressures become 
materially more persistent and tip the CPI sustainably above 6%. 
Source: National sources, CEIC, ING  
/ 
Balance of payments composition vs. Brent oil price  External balance: Buffers reinforced by oil

As on the fiscal side, higher oil prices have materially improved the 
external outlook, reversing much of the deterioration seen in 2025. 
As each US$10/bbl of oil price adds annual exports of around 4% of 
GDP, in 2026F the current account surplus may exceed 10% of GDP. 
In the meantime, sovereign assets equivalent to 115% of GDP keep 
growing and continue to provide substantial protection against 
potential external shocks. These buffers remain the main support for 
the AZN peg and broader macro stability. Over the longer term, 
however, declining hydrocarbon production and limited export 
diversification risk is likely to gradually erode the external surplus. 
Azerbaijan remains one of the region’s strongest external stories, 
although commodity dependence continues to shape its medium-
term outlook.  
Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Macro trend Political cycle Ratings FC LC 
Activity + Presidential: 2026 S&P BBB+ BBB+ 
Fiscal Loose Parliamentary: 2030 Moody’s Baa1 Baa1 
Monetary Loose Local: 2027 Fitch BBB+ BBB+

Bulgaria enters the post-euro period with a stronger institutional 
and financial anchor, but also with a less comfortable 
macroeconomic mix. Domestic demand remains strong, supported 
by rapid wage growth, a tight labour market and robust public 
investments. At the same time, elevated inflation, accelerating 
credit and widening twin deficits expose the trade-offs from an 
increasingly procyclical policy stance. The alignment of bank 
reserve requirements with euro-area standards has also improved 
domestic funding conditions, supporting financial deepening but 
increasing the importance of macroprudential policy as credit 
growth and property prices accelerate. Low public debt remains an 
important buffer, but recurring expenditure commitments are 
gradually narrowing the margin for error.  
*Quarterly data is eop. annual is average; **ECB Depo Rate as of 1 January 2026 
Shading denotes ING forecast data 
Source: National sources. ING estimates

Real GDP (%YoY) and contributions (ppt)  Growth remains resilient, but increasingly unbalanced

Economic activity has carried solid momentum into the second half of 
the year, with private consumption and investment continuing to 
drive growth. However, much of the strength in domestic demand is 
leaking into imports, while exports and industrial production remain 
weak. Household spending should continue to benefit from strong 
wage growth and a tight labour market, while the final phase of RRF 
implementation should support public investment. Even so, growth is 
likely to moderate as inflation erodes real income gains, fiscal support 
becomes less expansionary and rising household debt gradually 
constrains consumption. The key question is whether the public-
investment push can generate a stronger private-investment cycle. 
Source: NSI, ING

Inflation (%YoY) and main components (ppt)  Inflation: Services pressures remain the main concern

Inflation remains elevated, with persistent services pressures, higher 
energy costs and strong domestic demand offsetting the more 
recent easing in food inflation. Tight labour-market conditions 
continue to push up labour costs, while the euro changeover may 
have generated some rounding and repricing effects, although 
these do not appear to be the main driver. Disinflation should 
resume as demand growth normalises, fiscal policy becomes less 
supportive and the revised minimum-wage framework moderates 
wage pressures. The outlook nevertheless remains exposed to 
second-round effects from the fiscal impulse and to the risk of a 
more prolonged energy shock. 
Source: NSI, ING

BGARIA EUR credit spreads vs POLAND (bp)  Sovereign credit: Scope for a rebound

While Bulgaria entered this year with optimism from the official 
entry into the Eurozone, underperformance in the market has been 
driven by political uncertainty, given the snap elections seen in April, 
along with fiscal deterioration. With the political situation seemingly 
stabilised for now and a more realistic fiscal path being drawn out in 
terms of gradual consolidation after this year’s budget deficit 
widening, we see scope for an improvement in performance 
heading into 2027. Current valuations offer space for compression 
against the likes of Poland and Croatia, along with the potential to 
retighten against Eurozone hopeful Hungary.  
Source: Refinitiv, ING  James Wilson, EM Sovereign Strategist

Forecast summary  Country strategy: Still in good shape 
 2Q26 3Q26F 4Q26F 1Q27F 2Q27F 2026F 2027F 
Real GDP (%YoY) 1.7 2.3 1.6 2.2 2.5 2.0 2.2 
HICP (%YoY)* 4.2 4.3 4.5 4.2 3.8 4.3 3.8

10yr yield (%)* 3.39 4.09 4.18 4.15 4.05 3.80 4.10

Macro trend Political cycle Ratings FC LC 
Activity + Presidential: 2029 S&P A A 
Fiscal Stimulative Parliamentary: 2028 Moody’s A3 A3 
Monetary Stimulative Local: 2029 Fitch A- A-

Croatia’s economy continues to outperform the euro area, 
supported by resilient consumption, a tight labour market and the 
final phase of RRF-funded investment. However, growth has slowed 
more visibly in 2026, as sticky inflation, rising labour costs and 
weaker tourism have taken their toll on activity. Fiscal policy 
remains supportive, although strong nominal revenues should keep 
the deficit close to 3% of GDP and allow public debt to decline 
gradually. At the same time, a widening current-account deficit 
highlights the growing gap between still-solid domestic demand 
and the economy’s external earning capacity. Political stability and 
a solid sovereign rating remain important buffers, while a key 
medium-term objective resides in the rotation of the RRF-driven 
investment and cohesion funding towards greater private 
investment. 
*Quarterly data is eop, annual is average; shading denotes ING forecast data  
Source: National sources, ING estimates

Real GDP (YoY%) and contributions (ppt)  Growth: Domestic demand remains supportive

Economic growth has lost momentum during 2026 and we now 
expect a GDP annual expansion of around 2.0% for the year, down 
from 3.4% in 2025. Household consumption has remained robust 
overall but showed signs of weakness in the second quarter, while 
investment continued to benefit from the final RRF absorption push 
and other EU-funded projects. Nevertheless, elevated inflation has 
been weighing on purchasing power, financing conditions have 
become less supportive, and net exports remain a drag on output. 
Growth should remain above the euro-area average, but the end of 
the RRF cycle means that sustaining investment and productivity 
gains will increasingly become more dependent on private-sector 
projects. Source: Eurostat, ING

Inflation and labour costs  Inflation: Easing only gradually

Inflation has eased from its spring peak but remains elevated, 
reflecting energy costs and vigorous wage growth, with pressures 
remaining particularly evident across domestic services. While the 
currently tight labour market continues to support household 
spending, it has also fuelled price pressures across the tourism, 
construction and other labour-intensive sectors, adding some 
headwinds to competitiveness. Tourism should remain an important 
buffer for the economy, although rising revenues increasingly reflect 
higher prices rather than stronger volumes. Looking ahead, inflation 
risks remain tilted to the upside, particularly from renewed energy-
price pressures, adverse weather effects on food prices and persistent 
second-round effects from wage growth and services inflation. 
Source: Eurostat, ING

CROATI EUR credit spreads vs POLAND (bp)  Sovereign credit: No news is good news

With a combination of political noise and fiscal pressures a key 
worry for much of Europe (both EM and DM), Croatia continues to 
stand out as something of a safe haven given its relative stability. 
Steady growth, a sharp reduction in government debt ratios (30% of 
GDP reduction since 2020) and solid EU inflows mean Croatia 
continues to see a strong fundamental profile. Valuations put the 
country as one of the tightest names in the EM space, and tighter 
than some of the more troubled names in Western Europe, with  
5-year paper offering around 20bp in z-spread, and around 60bp on 
the 10-year. This offers limited scope for further tightening, but 
Croatia should remain a solid low-beta option. 
Source: Refinitiv, ING  James Wilson, EM Sovereign Strategist

 David Havrlant, Chief Economist, Czech Republic

Forecast summary  Country strategy: Higher inflation amid slower expansion 
 2Q26 3Q26F 4Q26F 1Q27F 2Q27F 2026F 2027F 
Real GDP (%YoY) 1.8 1.7 1.5 2.0 2.3 1.8 2.3 
CPI (%YoY)* 1.5 2.5 3.6 3.5 4.0 2.2 3.4 
Policy interest rate (%) 3.75 3.75 4.00 4.00 4.00 4.00 4.00 
3m interest rate (%)* 3.8 3.9 4.2 4.2 4.2 3.8 4.2 
10yr yield (%)* 4.5 5.3 5.3 5.1 5.1 4.9 5.1 
USD/CZK* 21.0 21.1 21.8 21.8 21.8 21.1 21.8 
EUR/CZK* 24.2 24.3 24.4 24.3 24.3 24.3 24.3

Macro trend Political cycle Ratings FC LC 
Activity + Presidential: 2028 S&P AA- AA 
Fiscal Looser Parliamentary: 2029 Moody’s Aa3 Aa3 
Monetary Neutral Local: 2030 Fitch AA- AA-

The global negative supply shock has propelled inflation upward and 
is now also taking its toll on economic activity, delivering a textbook 
outcome. Renewed growth in regulated and food prices is set to 
follow punchy fuel price gains, as Hormuz second-round effects 
start to materialise. Real GDP growth is set to slow to a mediocre 
pace, while the surge in inflation is set to cap real wage growth at 
the year turn. That said, manufacturing is proving resilient and will 
lend support to economic activity. Firms’ profitability may erode due 
to rising input costs amid ample competition. The CNB is likely to opt 
for a tighter monetary policy, as the weaker koruna contributes to 
domestic price pressures via galloping import prices. Still, the currency 
is set to withstand potential bond market turmoil, as fragile French 
finances may cause the ECB to hesitate. The Czech fiscal position 
remains comparatively strong, despite some recent easing. *Quarterly data is eop, annual is avg. Shading denotes ING forecast data  Source: National sources, ING estimates

Inflation will take centre stage (% YoY)   Real purchasing power to come under pressure

Second round effects stemming from the Hormuz conflict are set to 
foster higher headline inflation over the coming quarters, with 
regulated and food prices flipping into annual growth at the year 
turn. The energy distributors have already started passing higher 
purchase prices on to customers and, with global oil and natural gas 
prices expected to remain elevated for longer, this is only set to gain 
momentum over the coming months. Food price tags are set to 
surge over the coming year, fuelled by higher fertilizer and transport 
costs. The sub-average crop yields in the Czech Republic and other 
European countries will only exacerbate price pressures. 
Furthermore, it is complicated to get Ukrainian crops to the market, 
given the low level of the Danube River and the Odesa port 
blockade. A weaker koruna only contributes to galloping import 
prices. Taking all this into account, we expect average headline 
inflation to strengthen from 2.2% this year to 3.4% next year. 
The punchier inflation will take its toll on real wage growth, which 
we expect to slow from a robust 4.5% on average this year to 2.6% 
next year. Such a tangible slowdown in wages would have profound 
consequences for domestic spending, as it will get tougher for some 
medium- and many low-income households to make ends meet. 
Spenders may hit the brakes for precautionary reasons or due to 
budgetary constraints in anticipation of price surges in essential 
areas of expenditure, such as electricity, heating or food. Moreover, 
significant price increases in these segments may readily feed into 
inflation expectations. 
We see an overall nominal wage gain of just below 6% in 2027, 
despite the plans for robust wage increases in the public sector of 
around 8% and an 11% rise in the minimum wage. The public sector 
accounts for up to a quarter of all employed. Firms are unlikely to be 
willing or able to proceed with upbeat wage rises with profitability 
coming under pressure. Input costs are surging, while the ability to 
raise end-prices is limited due to the intense global competition, 
especially from China. When profit margins erode, firms typically 
scale back investment plans and rein in wage growth. Against this 
backdrop, real GDP growth is set to slow to 1.8% this year before 
only gradually regaining momentum, provided the global 
environment does not deteriorate further. Given the inflation 
outlook and the weaker koruna, we expect the CNB to tighten 
monetary policy further. A somewhat looser fiscal stance may 
reinforce the case for a hawkish policy bias among Czech 
policymakers. 
Source: CNB, ING, Macrobond

### Page 37

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

37 
Czech Republic david.havrlant@ing.com

Upbeat property price dynamics set to cool down (% YoY)  Softer housing market to calm future core inflation

Annual house price growth is set to cool noticeably this year from 
an average of 16% in 2025. At the same time, construction output 
recorded its first year-on-year drop in August after returning 
positive numbers for almost two years. We expect this to have 
profound implications for the still punchy annual gains in imputed 
rents, which account for just over a fifth of the core inflation basket. 
Indeed, imputed rents are significantly affected by the cost of 
materials and labour in the construction sector, along with property 
price dynamics. We forecast some cooling in the housing market 
this year, though the timing of the pass-through into core inflation 
remains uncertain. House price changes seem to take effect almost 
immediately, while it can take a couple of months for other factors 
to filter through. Source: CZSO, Macrobond

Input costs surge as global competition intensifies (% YoY)  Pressured profitability curbs wage gains 
Firms’ profit margins have started to come under pressure, already 
weighing on confidence in September’s PM  release.  ndeed, energy 
prices for European manufacturers are significantly higher than 
those faced by their global competitors. At the same time, China 
continues to expand industrial capacity and is competing 
aggressively across a broad range of sectors. Investment plans and 
wage increases are first to come under scrutiny when profitability 
dwindles. European ETS1 emission allowances are adding to this 
pain and contributing to concerns about Europe’s industrial 
competitiveness and deindustrialisation. If ETS2 is fully 
implemented, we anticipate further pressure on SMEs in particular. 
Czech industry is moving onto firmer ground, though we believe 
that the boost from Hormuz-related pre-stocking may fade.

Source: CZSO, Macrobond

Import prices affect core inflation with a lag (% YoY)  Weaker koruna contributes to galloping import prices

Import prices have continued their upward trend on the back of 
surging energy and material prices following the escalation of the 
Ukrainian and Middle Eastern conflicts. The weakening domestic 
currency has also started to add to import price pressures, largely 
reflecting the recent sell-off in government bonds stimulated by the 
global bond market turmoil. In any case, import prices affect all 
price segments to some extent, and are likely to feed into core 
inflation with some lag. This adds to the upside risk of an expected 
inflation surge at the turn of the year, which we believe warrants 
close attention from the central bank. We saw an acceleration in 
goods prices in September’s CP  and expect more is to come in 
October. 
Source: CZSO, CNB, Macrobond

Real interest rates about to fade (%)  Higher rates needed to keep inflation in check

Surging inflation is set to keep real interest rates compressed until 
the middle of next year. Add in the weaker koruna and the general 
monetary policy stance does not appear restrictive. Given the slowing 
economy, the koruna’s key advantages - the punchy interest rate 
differential and growth outperformance relative to the eurozone - 
could lose their luster somewhat. Therefore, we anticipate that the 
CNB will raise rates in November and attach a 30% probability to 
another hike in December. Still, we do not see this as the start of a 
conventional hiking cycle, as the economy is set to continue 
operating below its potential. In fact, we see the equilibrium rate as 
higher than generally assumed, as geopolitical conflicts continue 
and keep resource costs structurally higher. The cost of capital must 
adjust accordingly if price stability is to be secured. Source: CNB, ING, Macrobond

Czech Republic Strategy

FX: Spot vs forward and INGF  FX strategy (with Frantisek Taborsky, EMEA FX & FI Strategist)

The koruna remains a remarkably stable currency within the EM and 
CEE space amid heightened geopolitical uncertainty, elevated 
energy prices and a strong US dollar. We believe the CZK remains a 
safe haven among its peers for investors. On the one hand, the 
economy remains resilient to global challenges and supply shocks. 
On the other, the CNB remains the most hawkish central bank in the 
region, a stance reflected in aggressive rate pricing that provides a 
degree of protection.  
The interest rate differential remains the primary driver for EUR/CZK, 
more so than energy prices or the strength of the US dollar relative 
to peers, in our view. We expect the CNB to adopt an even more 
hawkish stance in the coming months as our forecast for inflation 
heads above 4% in January. Conversely, a weaker economy and 
fiscal issues in the Eurozone are exerting downward pressure on the 
outlook for front-end rates. Overall, we believe the koruna will retain 
its safe-haven status within the CEE region and outperform its CEE 
peers in the face of persistent geopolitical uncertainty and high 
energy prices. 
We expect EUR/CZK to move closer to 24.00 into the year end. 
Source: Eikon, ING   
Foreign holders of CZGB (%)

Source: MinFin, ING

Local IRS curve (2s10s, bp)  Fixed Income strategy (Frantisek Taborsky, EMEA FX & FI Strategist)

The CNB reinforced market hawkish sentiment with an unexpected 
rate hike in June, making it the only central bank in CEE to tighten 
monetary conditions so far. Inflation has remained well-behaved to 
date, but this is expected to change by the end of the year and 
especially in the first quarter of next year, when our forecast sees 
inflation temporarily rising above 4%. 
The market is currently pricing in roughly 110bp of CNB rate hikes, 
which remains higher than our own forecast. On the other hand, we 
see few reasons for market pricing to ease. The front end is driven 
primarily by energy prices, which are heavily dependent on the 
geopolitical situation. Consequently, we anticipate that pricing will 
remain elevated, or that a hawkish CNB will give the market reason 
to price in even more hikes. Meanwhile, long-end rates are already 
reaching highs last seen during the Covid years. We believe curves 
will begin to invert, as happened during the previous hiking cycle. 
In the bond market, next year will bring an approximately 28% 
increase in gross CZGB issuance, driven by a combination of a larger 
public finance deficit and record-high maturities. At the same time, 
we anticipate a decision early next year to exclude the Czech 
Republic from the GBI-EM index (a benchmark for local-currency 
emerging-market government bonds), which would trigger 
significant foreign outflows from the market. This, combined with 
CNB rate hikes, makes us bearish on CZGBs.

Source: Refinitiv, ING

Macro trend Political cycle Ratings FC LC 
Activity + Presidential: 2027  S&P BBB- BBB- 
Fiscal Restrictive Parliamentary: 2030 Moody’s Baa2 Baa2 
Monetary Restrictive Local: 2029 Fitch BBB BBB

Hungary has weathered the recent market storms 
uncharacteristically well. The antidote is called the “road to the 
euro”. Policymakers have laid the foundations, and the project is 
entering its credibility-building phase. A credible medium-term fiscal 
plan, official talks on ERM II and progress towards the Maastricht 
criteria should put Hungary into a convergence corridor. At the end 
of that road, we see euro adoption in 2032 at the earliest. If all goes 
well, interest-rate convergence will follow, while keeping the forint 
stable remains a must. A 2-3% GDP growth and a 2-3% inflation 
environment in the years ahead provide solid macro foundations. 
Targeted measures will support consumption, EU funds and better 
sentiment will revive investment activity, while new export 
capacities strengthen external balances. Sovereign rating upgrades 
from 2027 would be the icing on the cake. *Quarterly data is eop, annual is avg. Shading denotes ING forecast data  Source: National sources, ING estimates

Contribution to YoY GDP growth (ppt)  Macro digest

In the first half of 2026, the Hungarian economy grew by 1.8% on a 
yearly basis, slightly stronger than we expected six months ago. The 
Hungarian economy has now expanded for five consecutive quarters 
on a quarterly basis, confirming that it has left behind the preceding 
three-and-a-half-year period of stagnation. However, the details 
leave us somewhat less satisfied with the quality of growth. On the 
production side, agriculture contracted sharply due to the drought 
and heatwaves. Construction fell due to a lack of both private and 
public projects, while manufacturing expanded, with services delivering 
modest growth. On the demand side, household consumption 
continued to rise at a fast pace, while investment fell by 3.8% YoY, 
continuing the decline since mid-2022. Hence, the growth structure 
remained heavily one-sided, with net exports acting as a significant 
drag mostly due to the booming import activity. 
Looking ahead, the short-term outlook remains fundamentally 
positive, but the recovery is unlikely to shift into a much higher gear 
immediately. We expect GDP growth of 1.9% in 2026, with 
consumption remaining the main engine, supported by rising real 
disposable income and improving consumer confidence. Investment 
could recover towards year-end as EU funds increasingly feed into 
activity, although the weak starting point remains a concern. At the 
same time, net exports are likely to weigh on growth this year. Further 
ahead, stronger domestic demand and an eventual recovery in 
external demand could push GDP growth towards 3% in 2027-28. 
However, nearly four years of stagnating capital stock, weak 
investment dynamics and adverse demographic trends increasingly 
limit the economy’s potential to sustain growth materially above 3% 
without creating internal or external imbalances. 
Inflation continues to deliver downside surprises. Headline inflation 
accelerated from 1.3% to just 1.6% YoY in September, significantly less 
than expected, despite a 5.9% monthly jump in fuel prices. The key 
message is the continued weakness of underlying price pressures: core 
inflation fell to 1.9% YoY, while food and services prices declined on a 
monthly basis. Despite the weaker forint, elevated wage costs and 
higher energy prices, there is still no sign of a broad-based wave of 
price increases. The new 2.5% inflation target from 2028 could 
increasingly anchor price-setting decisions and contain underlying 
price pressures. Nevertheless, accumulated cost pressures may still be 
passed on during the usual repricing period, pushing headline inflation 
towards 3.5% by next summer. We expect average inflation of 1.8% in 
2026 and around 2.8% in 2027. Domestic price developments remain 
consistent with further monetary easing, although external conditions 
will determine its timing. 
Source: Hungarian Central Statistical Office

Hungary peter.virovacz@ing.com

Budget and primary balance of general government (%)  Fiscal credibility faces a major test

The revised 2026 budget targets a deficit of 7.5% of GDP, although 
we see room for an overperformance of around 0.5ppt, mainly due 
to stronger consumption and strict expenditure control. We expect a 
credible, front-loaded medium-term consolidation, focused primarily 
on expenditure and without conventional austerity measures, 
complying with EDP rules. A declining yield environment should 
generate sizeable savings on debt servicing, complemented by less 
wasteful public spending. However, open questions remain on the 
road to euro adoption. Market-distorting windfall and sectoral taxes 
will need to be addressed, while the utility cost reduction scheme 
may also require an overhaul. Consolidation will be a balancing act, 
but a credible path could clearly strengthen the convergence story. 
Source: AMECO, ING estimates

Benchmark policy rate and interest rate corridor  Further monetary easing is still on the cards

The NBH kept the base rate at 5.50% in September, pressing the 
pause button after the summer’s mini rate-cut cycle. Policymakers 
have re-enabled a flexible, data-driven approach, leaving the door 
open for further easing. Domestic conditions certainly support this: 
subdued price pressures and improving inflation expectations are 
consistent with lower rates, while the new 2.5% target from 2028 
provides an additional anchor. The main constraint is the external 
environment, with forint vulnerability, elevated yields and energy 
prices still calling for caution. A credible fiscal path and progress 
towards ERM    could lower  ungary’s risk premium and create room 
to act. We see one more 25bp cut this year, taking the base rate to 
5.25%, followed by another 100bp of easing in 2027. 
Source: National Bank of Hungary

Unemployment and job vacancy (Beveridge-curve)  The labour market is cracking, but not breaking

The Hungarian labour market is showing signs of weakness again. In 
August, unemployment rose to 4.7%, while employment declined 
amid demographic pressures and workforce rationalisation. 
However, persistent supply-side constraints should limit large-scale 
layoffs. We maintain our forecast for average unemployment of 
around 4.5% in 2026, with upside risks from prolonged geopolitical 
uncertainty. Wage growth is gradually slowing, but real wage 
growth remains exceptionally strong. The planned 14% minimum 
wage hike for 2027 looks unsustainable; we expect a rise of around 
5%, complemented by personal income tax cuts for below-median 
earners. Longer term, structural labour shortages and continued 
wage convergence will increasingly force companies to boost 
efficiency and productivity. 
Source: Hungarian Central Statistical Office, ING estimates

Structure of the current account (% of GDP)  External balances to become an FX stabiliser

External balances have remained resilient despite the global energy 
shock from geopolitics and local weather. The ramp-up of new 
industrial capacities is increasingly supporting goods exports, while 
services exports continue to expand strongly. Nevertheless, we 
expect the current account to slip into a small deficit this year 
before gradually returning to surplus in the coming years. The 
financing side should become increasingly supportive as well, with 
recovering FDI inflows and EU funds significantly improving 
 ungary’s net external financing capacity. Together with stronger 
export capacity, this should allow the already record-high FX 
reserves to rise further. This would strengthen a much-needed line 
of defence on the road to a potential ERM II entry in 2029. 
Source: National Bank of Hungary, ING estimates

Hungary Strategy

FX: Spot and INGF  FX strategy (with Frantisek Taborsky, EMEA FX & FI Strategist)

EUR/HUF has shown some volatility recently, but the swings have 
been far more moderate than in the past. Positioning and the euro 
adoption story provide some insulation from global market 
turbulence, although the forint remains sensitive to energy prices 
and broader risk sentiment. The NB ’s focus on FX stability and the 
pause in its easing cycle also provide support. 
The next major catalyst should be domestic. We expect the 2027 
budget and the medium-term fiscal consolidation plan to provide a 
credibility boost, especially if accompanied by official talks on 
joining ERM II. A credible package could significantly reduce 
 ungary’s risk premium and take EUR/ UF towards the 350-355 
area in the short term. Improving external balances and rising FX 
reserves should add another layer of support. Such a move, 
combined with the benign inflation backdrop, would give the NBH 
more room to ease and pave the way for a December rate cut. 
However, we do not expect the appreciation trend to continue 
indefinitely. Once monetary easing resumes, the narrowing rate 
advantage should put the HUF on a mild depreciation path over the 
subsequent 12 months. We expect a 25bp cut in December and 
another 100bp of easing in 2027. Yet, with the risk premium 
structurally lower, we see limited room for a major reversal. This 
would make 350-355 more of a stepping stone than a new 
equilibrium. We see EUR/HUF settling around 360-365 as we 
approach a possible ERM II entry in 2029. 
Source: National Bank of Hungary, ING estimates  
Evolution of gross external debt (% of GDP)

Source: National Bank of Hungary, ING estimates

Local curve (%)  Fixed income strategy (with Frantisek Taborsky, EMEA FX & FI

Strategist and James Wilson, EM Sovereign Strategist) 
Hungary remains one of the most crowded trades within emerging 
markets, and there is certainly further potential for spread 
compression relative to peers and core markets. However, the main 
risks are crowded long positioning as well as the amount of good 
news is already priced in. At the same time, global factors are once 
again playing a role in the Hungarian market, and we have seen 
sell-offs as well across HUF assets since the April elections. We 
remain bullish on the local story but are looking for better entry 
levels to re-establish previous exposure. In particular, long-end HGB 
and rate spreads versus the euro should see further compression, 
and we expect continued curve flattening here, despite levels 
already being deeply inverted. 
While the overall story remains positive for Hungary, we continue to 
see less value in the Eurobond market, relative to local markets. FX 
issuance for the year has been completed, while front-loading 
supply early in 2027 is likely. The composition of issuance could shift 
slightly more towards EUR as the country moves closer to euro 
adoption. As with local markets, a strong and credible budget plan 
could offer more scope for spreads to compress relative to higher-
rated peers. However, expectations are already elevated for 
significant consolidation to be announced, and valuations do not 
offer much scope for upside. REP UN €40s have seen spreads 
compress by 90bp YT  relative to BGAR A €38s, and the pickup for 
Hungary in general is now in the range of 10-20bp over Bulgaria and 
20-40bp over Poland. 
Source: Government Debt Management Agency  
Public debt redemption profile (end-Jun 2026, HUFbn)

Source: Government Debt Management Agency

Macro trend Political cycle Ratings FC LC 
Activity + Presidential: 2029 S&P BBB BBB 
Fiscal Neutral Parliamentary: 2031 Moody’s Baa1 Baa1 
Monetary Tight Local: n/a Fitch BBB BBB

Kazakhstan continues to benefit from one of the strongest sovereign 
and external positions in the CCA region, supported by sizeable 
sovereign assets, a balanced network of international partners and 
higher oil prices. More recently, strong portfolio inflows given still-
attractive real rates, progress towards Euroclear connectivity, 
growing domestic debt supply and positive rating momentum have 
supported the tenge and local markets. In addition, a sizeable 
backlog of future state FX sales provides an important buffer against 
potential market stress. At the same time, the longer-term outlook 
remains constrained by persistent twin deficits, limited private-
sector investment and capital inflows and an uneven track record in 
fiscal discipline and inflation targeting. Current CPI trend leaves 
room for a moderate easing, but the trajectory is facing upside risks. *Quarterly data is eop, annual is avg. Shading denotes ING forecast data  
Source: National sources, ING estimates

GDP growth and major contributors (%YoY)  Activity: Strong growth despite oil disruptions VAT hike

GDP growth has slowed this year compared to the exceptional pace 
seen in 2025 but remains robust despite the VAT hike and oil-related 
disruptions. Non-oil sectors continue to perform relatively well, while 
higher oil prices support confidence and improve the fiscal and 
external outlook. Domestic demand also appears supported by 
quasi-fiscal stimulus estimated at 4-5% of GDP. At the same time, 
retail and corporate lending are showing signs of cooling amid tight 
monetary conditions. Headline fiscal policy has entered a 
consolidation phase, reflected in lower reliance on NFRK transfers to 
finance current spending, although a durable track record of fiscal 
discipline has yet to be established. 
Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Inflation, key rate, and 12M CPI expectations (%)  Monetary policy: Real rate to remain high

The moderate easing in inflation from its 2025 peaks and a resilient 
tenge have allowed the NBK to start cutting the base rate in 2026. 
Inflation pressures from the VAT hike and global commodity-price 
spike have remained under control for now, while portfolio inflows 
and attractive real yields have given an additional boost to the 
currency. At the same time, tariff increases, fiscal and quasi-fiscal 
stimulus, and producer-price pressures amid global inflation risks 
continue to justify our above-consensus CPI outlook. Therefore, we 
expect the easing cycle to continue only gradually, while remaining 
alert to potential external inflation shocks. Although Kazakhstan 
retains room for nominal easing, real policy rates are likely to 
remain high by regional standards and supportive of capital inflows. Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

Balance of payments (US$ bn) and USDKZT  External balance: Portfolio flows increasingly matter

Higher oil prices have strengthened Kazakhstan's external position, 
although part of the benefit is offset by weaker export volumes. The 
country continues to run structural current-account deficits as 
strong imports and dividend outflows offset export revenues. As a 
result, capital flows are becoming increasingly important for KZT 
dynamics. Progress towards Euroclear connectivity, positive rating 
momentum and additional public and corporate debt issuance 
should continue attracting portfolio inflows amid high real rates, 
supporting the tenge. We have improved our KZT outlook but note 
that greater reliance on portfolio capital in an economy with 
persistent twin deficits also increases sensitivity to shifts in global risk 
appetite and flow reversals. 
Source: National sources, CEIC, ING

 Rafał Benecki, Chief Economist, Poland

Forecast summary   Country strategy: Robust growth and fragile fiscal 
 2Q26 3Q26F 4Q26F 1Q27F 2Q27F 2026F 2027F

USD/PLN* 3.74 3.84 3.85 3.83 3.74 3.73 3.75

Macro trend Political cycle Ratings FC LC 
Activity – Presidential: 2029 S&P A- A 
Fiscal Loose Parliamentary: 2027  Moody’s A3 A3 
Monetary Neutral Local: 2028 Fitch A- A-

Poland is set to remain one of the fastest-growing EU economies in 
2027, with public and private investment increasingly complementing 
consumption. Foreign investor exposure to equity and bonds reflects 
optimism on growth but concern on the fiscal side, with non-residents 
accounting for 80% of equity trading but only 12% of the POLGB 
stock. The government is pursuing a supply side agenda to support 
innovation in the economy, but further structural reforms are needed, 
particularly on fiscal policy, with a 2025-27 deficit of c.7% of GDP. 
We don’t see fiscal adjustment in the 2027 election year but expect 
fewer and later NBP hikes (50bp in 1Q27 vs 100bp priced). In our 
view, elevated hike expectations overstate CPI risks as they also 
reflect a deterioration of the fiscal trajectory. Hike expectations may 
quickly compress, if energy prices reverse, but fiscal risks should stay 
elevated in the coming years as the combination of 2027 election 
uncertainty and a veto prone President creates risk of a downgrade 
(1-2 notches downgrade already priced) and is PLN negative.  
*Quarterly data is eop, annual is avg, Shading denotes ING estimates 
Source: National sources, ING estimates

GDP composition (%YoY)  Investment increasingly complementing consumption

Both economic growth and consumer inflation proved remarkably 
resilient to the energy shock triggered by the outbreak of war in the 
Middle East. GDP growth approached 4% YoY in 2Q26, probably 
marking the peak of the current economic cycle. Headline CPI 
inflation rose but was contained by a fuel price freeze while the 
absence of second-round effects kept core inflation under control. 
At the same time, the authorities maintained an expansionary fiscal 
stance, resulting in a rapid build-up of public debt. Debt may soon 
reach the 55% of GDP threshold (local definition), which, under the 
current public finance law, could require an abrupt fiscal adjustment 
in 2029. The recent deterioration in the fiscal trajectory together 
with political gridlock between the government and the president, 
led Moody’s to downgrade Poland’s sovereign rating to A3 with a 
stable outlook. Fitch maintains a negative outlook, while S&P is due 
to review the rating soon. 
The energy crisis brought Poland’s easing cycle in monetary policy 
to a halt, and a prolonged period of elevated inflation could prompt 
hikes, albeit later and of a lesser magnitude than priced, with our 
forecast pointing to 1Q27. If a prolonged energy shock does 
eventually lead to a rise in core inflation and other central banks 
continue to tighten policy, the zloty could come under pressure. 
While inflation is generally projected to moderate in the second half 
of next year, the National Bank of Poland (NBP) may seek to ensure 
that broader inflationary pressures and second-round effects do not 
emerge from the current energy crisis. The rise in Polish government 
bond yields has been driven mainly by global developments and the 
repricing of US and European sovereign debt. 
We continue to expect GDP growth of 3.4% in 2026 and 3.2% in 
2027, supported by public investments financed by RRF and 
cohesion funds, alongside a recent acceleration in private outlays. 
Household consumption should remain the main driver of economic 
expansion, but its pace is likely to moderate from 2025 as higher 
petrol prices and slower wage growth weigh on real disposable 
income. However, savings accumulated in previous years should 
provide a cushion to consumers spending. 
We expect fiscal policy to remain loose during the 2027 election 
year, with any consolidation efforts postponed. However, we note 
the fiscal debate has shifted from a focus on fiscal spending towards 
optimisation of both sides of the budget. Future austerity measures 
will probably be needed on both the revenue and expenditure sides 
of the budget. 
Source: Polish Statistical Office, ING forecasts

### Page 47

Directional Economics CEEMEA – Central Europe’s new horizons: from investment hosts to global investors October 2026

47 
Poland 13Brafal.benecki@ing.pl

CPI inflation and its composition (%, percentage points)   Fuel interventions set up a bumpy CPI path in 2027

CPI inflation reached 4.0% YoY in September, moving above the 
upper bound of the central bank’s 2.5% target range (±1 percentage 
point). However, the increase is mainly attributable to higher fuel 
prices. Government intervention, through temporary reductions in 
excise duty and VAT on fuel (2Q26, second half of August, 4Q26), is 
generating base effects for the 2027 inflation path.  
Headline inflation should moderate towards the National Bank of 
Poland’s (NBP) target in 2 27, although its composition is likely to 
change markedly. We expect lower core inflation and fuel price 
deflation (strong base effects) to be offset by above-average food 
inflation due to a poorer harvest, higher fertiliser prices and a low 
reference base following the current period of food price deflation.  
Source: Polish Statistical Office, ING forecasts

Fiscal deficit evolution 2021-25 (% of GDP)  Fiscal policy to stay loose until elections

Higher social spending, substantial increases in public-sector wages, 
elevated defence expenditure and rising debt-servicing costs have 
pushed the fiscal deficit above 7% of GDP in recent years. In 2026, 
the general government deficit is also expected to remain close to 
7% of GDP, while the draft budget bill for the 2027 election year 
similarly envisages an imbalance of around 7% of GDP.  
There are currently no credible fiscal consolidation plans in the 
pipeline, with the necessary adjustment effectively postponed until 
after the elections, to beyond 2027. The lack of cooperation 
between PM  onald Tusk’s government and President Karol 
Nawrocki further complicates the implementation of fiscal reforms. 
While the president has criticised the excessive imbalance, he has 
also pledged to veto tax hikes and spending cuts.  Source: Eurostat, ING

Public debt, % of GDP, ESA2010  Rising public debt could force an abrupt fiscal adjustment

High fiscal deficits, combined with non-market borrowing under the 
EU’s Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) and Security Action for 
Europe (SAFE), are driving a rapid build-up of public debt. The local 
definition of debt (which differs from the ESA definition) is 
approaching the statutory threshold that, if breached, will require a 
significant fiscal adjustment. 
As early as 2027, public debt under the domestic definition could 
flirt with 55% of GDP threshold set out in the Public Finance Act. 
Breaching this threshold next year would require either a balanced 
budget on a cash basis in 2029 or a deficit consistent with a declining 
debt-to-GDP ratio. While the fiscal rules could be amended, such a 
move is likely to be viewed negatively by rating agencies and 
investors. Moreover, the Polish Constitution prohibits public debt 
from exceeding 60% of GDP, and securing the constitutional 
majority required to amend this provision appears unlikely. 
Source: Polish Statistical Office, ING forecasts

Current account and trade balance (% of GDP)  Trade gap widens but external balance remains solid

Due to high energy commodity prices and elevated deliveries of 
military equipment, Poland’s external gap is expected to widen to 
1.5% of GDP in 2026 and further to 1.9% of GDP in 2027, from a 
relatively low -1.0% of GDP in 2025. This should reflect a widening 
trade deficit, also from relatively low levels, while the large income 
balance gap should be partly offset by a solid surplus in services. 
In merchandise trade turnover, China has recently overtaken 
Germany to become Poland’s largest source of imports (based on 
monthly data). Rising imports are being driven by robust domestic 
demand but are also partly re-exported to Western Europe. Polish 
exporters are proving resilient despite weak European demand and 
rising Chinese competition in both intermediate and final goods.  
Source: NBP, ING forecasts
